5.2
Statistical Uncertainties

The statistical uncertainty on
R_{n/p}^{sf}
is directly taken from the fit parameter error provided by CERN ROOT. This error is estimated using the covariance matrix derived from chi-squared minimization, which accounts for any correlations between the fit parameters. It is then propagated to
R
as follows:

\Delta(R)_{stat}=|R_{MC}^{Born}|\,\Delta(R_{n/p}^{sf})_{stat}

(5.3)

The statistical uncertainty on
R_{MC}^{Born}
is zero, as it is known exactly. Finally, the statistical error on
G_{M}^{n}
is evaluated using:

\displaystyle\Delta(G_{M}^{n})_{stat}=\frac{1}{2}\left|\frac{1}{G_{M}^{n}}\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}\frac{\epsilon_{n}(1+\tau_{n})}{\epsilon_{p}(1+\tau_{p})}\sigma^{p}_{Red}\right|\Delta(R)_{stat}

(5.4)

with the assumptions:
\Delta(\sigma^{p}_{Red})_{stat}=0
and
\Delta(G_{E}^{n})_{stat}=0
.

5.3
Systematic Uncertainties

Given the availability of sufficient statistics, the overall precision of the E12-09-019 measurements will be primarily determined by systematic uncertainties. Fortunately, the ratio method of measurement minimizes many systematic errors, including DAQ live time, trigger efficiency, electron track reconstruction efficiency, and target density. The remaining significant sources of systematic uncertainty and the methods used to quantify them are discussed in this section.

5.3.1
Inelastic Contamination

The contamination from inelastic scattering within the quasi-elastic event sample is significant, even for the lowest-
Q^{2}
dataset, making it the largest source of systematic uncertainty in E12-09-019 measurements. Since the “true” shape of this background in the
\Delta{x}
distribution is unknown, it cannot be precisely subtracted from the data. However, as the underlying distribution is smooth and symmetric, it can be effectively modeled and subtracted, as detailed in Sections
4.6.1
and
4.6.2
. For each dataset, the data/MC fit to extract
R_{n/p}^{sf}
is repeated multiple times, using consistent signal shapes but varying background models. Specifically, five different background models, listed in the above-mentioned sections, are employed. The consistency of the extracted
R_{n/p}^{sf}
values across these fits reflects how accurately the inelastic background was modeled. Therefore, the standard deviation of these values is quoted as the systematic uncertainty due to inelastic contamination. The propagation of this error to
R
and
G_{M}^{n}
is straightforward and follows Equations
5.3
and
5.4
, respectively. The error numbers are summarized in Table
5.2
.

5.3.2
HCAL Detection Efficiency

Any discrepancy in the description of HCAL nucleon detection efficiency (NDE) between data and MC affects the extraction of
R_{n/p}^{sf}
, necessitating the quantification of associated uncertainty as part of the systematic error estimation. As discussed in Section
4.5
, a rigorous analysis was performed to determine the uniformity of proton detection efficiency within HCAL acceptance using elastic
H(e,e^{\prime}p)
events. Based on these findings, an efficiency map was created to capture efficiency differences within HCAL acceptance effectively. Using this map, the relative weights of the MC events were adjusted to replicate the efficiency variation observed in the data. With this efficiency correction, the efficiency profile observed in the data could be effectively reproduced in the otherwise uniform MC. The difference in
R_{n/p}^{sf}
values extracted with and without the efficiency correction provides an upper bound on the uncertainty related to HCAL NDE uniformity and is thus quoted as the corresponding systematic uncertainty.

The efficiency map used to address HCAL NDE non-uniformity is based solely on proton detection efficiency (pDE) estimated from data, as no straightforward method exists to directly measure neutron detection efficiency (nDE) from E12-09-019 data. Although the strong agreement between MC and data for pDE provides confidence that MC reliably represents nDE within the required error margin, it is still necessary to establish an upper bound on the uncertainty of HCAL nDE. Additionally, the inability to calculate HCAL proton detection efficiency reliably at higher
Q^{2}
kinematics due to significant inelastic background introduces further uncertainty that also requires careful quantification. In this work, a 2
\%
systematic error on
R_{n/p}^{sf}
has been assigned across all kinematic settings to account for these uncertainties based on informed estimates, though dedicated analysis efforts are ongoing for a more precise evaluation.

5.3.3
Cut Stability

As discussed in Section
4.6.3
, the optimal set of analysis cuts was determined by examining the stability of the experimental observable as a function of the cut variables. A systematic approach, guided by both data and MC, was employed to identify the stability region. However, this region is more distinct for some cut variables than for others. The diminishing signal-to-background ratio at higher-
Q^{2}
further complicates this determination, making it more uncertain. Quantifying the associated uncertainty is crucial as part of the systematic error in measuring
R_{n/p}^{sf}
.

Figure 5.1
:
Distribution of
R_{n/p}^{sf}
for all E12-09-019 kinematics, resulting from the cut stability study. “Poly2” background model has been used to perform these extractions.

The approach involves varying the range (both lower and upper separately, if applicable) of each cut variable by
\pm 10\%
while keeping the other cuts at their optimized values. For each variation, a data/MC fit to the
\Delta{x}
distribution is performed to extract
R_{n/p}^{sf}

1
1

1

Care was taken to ensure that the cuts were varied equivalently between data and MC signals.
. The standard deviation of the resulting
R_{n/p}^{sf}
distribution reflects the spread of the data due to sensitivity to the analysis cuts, thereby quantifying the associated uncertainty. All cut variables except for
N_{hit}^{GEM}
,
t_{coin}^{ADC}
, and
Size_{clus}^{GRINCH}
listed in Table
4.3
were varied for a given kinematic setting to determine the systematic uncertainty due to cut stability. Figure
5.1
shows the distribution of
R_{n/p}^{sf}
values, determined with the “Poly2” background model, resulting from the cut stability studies across all E12-09-019 kinematics. In each case, the distributions approximately follow a Gaussian shape, as expected.

5.3.4
Final State Interaction

In quasi-elastic electron-deuteron scattering, the struck nucleon can interact with the spectator nucleon, a phenomenon known as final state interaction (FSI). FSIs lead to significant modifications in the momentum and angular distributions of outgoing nucleons, affecting the cross-section. However, because this effect is largely similar for neutrons and protons, it nearly cancels out in the cross-section ratio. Nevertheless, it is necessary to calculate the associated correction factor—larger at lower momentum transfers due to stronger inter-nucleon interactions—using theoretical models and apply it to the extracted ratio.

SIMC, the quasi-elastic event generator used for E12-09-019 analysis, does not include models for FSIs; therefore, such corrections must be calculated and applied separately. Initial discussions with theorists suggest a correction factor of approximately
0.5\%
on
R
for low-
Q^{2}
kinematics. Further efforts are ongoing to accurately compute correction factors for high-
Q^{2}
data points. In this work, a
0.5\%
systematic error on
R
has been assigned across all kinematic settings to account for FSI-related corrections.

5.3.5
\sigma_{Red}^{p}
Estimation

Proton reduced cross section (
\sigma_{Red}^{p}
) is evaluated using multiple models for a given kinematics to estimate the associated systematic uncertainty. This process involves calculating
G_{M}^{n}
from
R
using three different models for
\sigma_{Red}^{p}
while keeping the
G_{E}^{n}
value fixed to the one obtained from Ye
et al.
. The models used are:

1.

Ye
et al.

[
51
]
,

2.

Kelly
et al.

[
158
]
, and

3.

Arrington
et al.

[
161
]

The standard deviation of the resulting
G_{M}^{n}
values is then quoted as the associated systematic uncertainty for
\sigma_{Red}^{p}
estimation.

It is worth noting that the models used in this work include corrections for the two-photon exchange (TPE) cross-section in
ep
scattering. The ideal approach, however, would be to use a TPE-uncorrected model to estimate
\sigma_{Red}^{p}
and then multiply it by
R
to obtain the TPE-uncorrected Born cross-section for
en
scattering. This result could then be corrected for the TPE contribution to yield the neutron Born cross-section. Finally, the “true”
G_{M}^{n}
value can be extracted from the neutron Born cross-section by applying the
G_{E}^{n}
correction. Efforts are ongoing to implement this approach.

5.3.6
G_{E}^{n}
Estimation

The same procedure used to estimate systematic uncertainly on
\sigma_{Red}^{p}
is used here as well.
G_{M}^{n}
is calculated from
R
using the following three models for
G_{E}^{n}
while keeping the
\sigma_{Red}^{p}
value fixed to the one obtained from Ye
et al.
:

1.

Ye
et al.

[
51
]

2.

Kelly
et al.

[
158
]
, and

3.

Galster
et al.

[
162
]

The standard deviation of the resulting
G_{M}^{n}
values is then quoted as the associated systematic uncertainty for
G_{E}^{n}
estimation.

5.4
Discussion of the Results

Table
5.3
presents the preliminary
R
and
G_{M}^{n}
values, along with associated statistical and systematic uncertainties, for all E12-09-019 kinematics. The systematic errors on
R
dominate the overall uncertainty across all kinematics except for
Q^{2}=9.9

\text{\,}\mathrm{(}\mathrm{G}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}\mathrm{/}\mathrm{c}\mathrm{)}^{2}
, where statistical error prevails due to relatively low statistics. This lower data yield was caused by a significant loss of beam time during the experimental run. Notably, the quoted systematic uncertainties due to HCAL neutron detection efficiency and final state interactions (FSIs) are currently based on educated estimates. Efforts are underway to refine the quantification of these effects.

Figure 5.2
:

G_{M}^{n}
world data, including the preliminary results from this work. The error bars represent the total error, calculated by combining statistical and systematic errors in quadrature. The global fit is sourced from
[
51
]
. Among the theory curves, the one by Gross et al.
[
163
]
is based on the covariant spectator model, Lomon et al. on the vector meson dominance (VMD) model, and Diehl on GPD calculations. See Section
2.2.2
for a detailed overview of the
G_{M}^{n}
world data.

Additionally, the two-photon exchange (TPE) contribution to the
eD
scattering cross-section has been omitted in the calculation of the
G_{M}^{n}
values presented here, but efforts are ongoing to quantify these corrections, which are expected to be non-negligible.

When plotting the obtained
G_{M}^{n}
values alongside existing world data, as shown in Figure
5.2
, interesting insights emerge. The preliminary results at lower
Q^{2}
points closely align with existing world data, while the results at higher
Q^{2}
greatly extend the range in which
G_{M}^{n}
is precisely known. The observed trend suggests a slower falloff of
G_{M}^{n}/\mu_{n}G_{D}
than previously anticipated, implying a more compact neutron structure. The model by Gross et al.
[
163
]
, published in 2008, appears to most closely capture this observed trend. Their calculation is based on the covariant spectator model, where the virtual photon interacts with a single quark while the remaining quarks are treated as an on-shell diquark with a definite mass. A comprehensive interpretation of these results will likely necessitate significant refinement of existing nucleon models and parametrizations, thereby enhancing our understanding of the neutron’s internal structure.

Appendix A
Supplementary Derivations for the Rosenbluth Formula

This section will provide detailed intermediate steps in the derivation of the Rosenbluth formula presented in Section
1.2
.

A.1
Useful Identities

Minkowski Metric Tensor:

\displaystyle g=\text{diag}(1,

\displaystyle-1,-1,-1)

(A.1)

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=4

Gamma Matrices:

\displaystyle\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=2g^{\mu\nu}

(A.2)

Traces:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(A+B)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}(A)+\text{Tr}(B)

(A.3)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(\alpha A)

\displaystyle=\alpha\text{Tr}(A)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(AB)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}(BA)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu})

\displaystyle=g^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\lambda}\gamma^{\sigma})

\displaystyle=4\left(g^{\mu\nu}g^{\lambda\sigma}-g^{\mu\lambda}g^{\nu\sigma}+g^{\mu\sigma}g^{\nu\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\text{The trace of the product of an}

\displaystyle\text{odd number of gamma matrices in zero.}

Casimir’s Trick:

\sum_{\text{all spins}}[\bar{u}(a)\Gamma_{1}u(b)][\bar{u}(a)\Gamma_{2}u(b)]=\text{Tr}[\Gamma_{1}(\not{p_{b}}+m_{b})\bar{\Gamma}_{2}(\not{p_{a}}+m_{a})],\quad\text{where}\,\,\bar{\Gamma}_{2}=\gamma^{0}\Gamma_{2}^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}

(A.4)

Lab Frame Kinematics (see Section
1.2.2
):

\displaystyle q^{2}

\displaystyle=(k-k^{\prime})^{2}=-2k\cdot k^{\prime}=-2M(E_{e}-E^{\prime}_{e})=-4E_{e}E^{\prime}_{e}\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}

(A.5)

\displaystyle=(p^{\prime}-p)^{2}=2M^{2}-2p\cdot p^{\prime}

\displaystyle k\cdot q

\displaystyle=-k^{\prime}\cdot q=\frac{q^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle k\cdot p

\displaystyle=k^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}=ME_{e}

\displaystyle k\cdot p^{\prime}

\displaystyle=k^{\prime}\cdot p=ME_{e}+\frac{q^{2}}{2}

A.2
Hadronic Tensor

The hadronic tensor (
W^{N}_{\mu\nu}
) is defined as follows (see Equation
1.2.1
):

\displaystyle W^{N}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r^{\prime},r}[\bar{u}^{r^{\prime}}(p^{\prime})(-ie\Gamma_{\mu})u^{r}(p)][\bar{u}^{r^{\prime}}(p^{\prime})(-ie\Gamma_{\nu})u^{r}(p)]^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\text{Tr}[\Gamma_{\mu}(\not{p}+M)\Gamma_{\nu}(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)]\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:casimirstrick}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\text{Tr}\left[\left(\gamma_{\mu}(F_{1}+F_{2})-\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}F_{2}\right)(\not{p}+M)\left(\gamma_{\nu}(F_{1}+F_{2})-\frac{p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu}}{2M}F_{2}\right)(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\text{Tr}\left[(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\gamma_{\mu}(\not{p}+M)\gamma_{\nu}(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)-(F_{1}+F_{2})F_{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}(\not{p}+M)\gamma_{\nu}(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.-(F_{1}+F_{2})F_{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu}}{2M}\gamma_{\mu}(\not{p}+M)(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)+F_{2}^{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu}}{2M}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}(\not{p}+M)(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\text{Tr}\left[(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\gamma_{\mu}(\not{p}+M)\gamma_{\nu}(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)-2(F_{1}+F_{2})F_{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}(\not{p}+M)\gamma_{\nu}(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+F_{2}^{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu}}{2M}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}(\not{p}+M)(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)\right]

\displaystyle=(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\frac{1}{2}\,\text{Tr}[\gamma_{\mu}(\not{p}+M)\gamma_{\nu}(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)]-2(F_{1}+F_{2})F_{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}\frac{1}{2}\,\text{Tr}[(\not{p}+M)\gamma_{\nu}(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)]

\displaystyle\quad+F_{2}^{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu}}{2M}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}\frac{1}{2}\,\text{Tr}[(\not{p}+M)(\not{p^{\prime}}+M)]

\displaystyle=(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\,2\,[p_{\mu}p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p^{\prime}_{\mu}p_{\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})]-2(F_{1}+F_{2})F_{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}2M(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})

\displaystyle\quad+F_{2}^{2}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu}}{2M}\frac{p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}}{2M}2(p\cdot p^{\prime}+M^{2})\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:traceidentities}]

\displaystyle=2\,\bigg\{\bigg.(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}[p_{\mu}p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p^{\prime}_{\mu}p_{\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})]-(F_{1}+F_{2})F_{2}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{F_{2}^{2}}{4M^{2}}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})(p\cdot p^{\prime}+M^{2})\bigg.\bigg\}

\displaystyle=2\,\left[(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\mathcal{A}-(F_{1}F_{2}+F_{2}^{2})\mathcal{B}+\frac{F_{2}^{2}}{4M^{2}}\mathcal{B}(2M^{2}-\frac{q^{2}}{2})\right]

(A.6)

where
\mathcal{A}=p_{\mu}p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p^{\prime}_{\mu}p_{\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})
and
\mathcal{B}=(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})
. In the last line, we have used the relation
p\cdot p^{\prime}+M^{2}=2M^{2}-\frac{q^{2}}{2}
, assuming lab frame kinematics (see
(
A.5
)
).

Next, we replace
F_{1}F_{2}
with
\frac{1}{2}[(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}-F_{1}^{2}-F_{2}^{2}]
, followed by some straightforward rearrangements. This leads to the simplified expression for
W^{N}_{\mu\nu}
in the lab frame, as follows:

\displaystyle W_{\mu\nu}^{N}

\displaystyle=(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})\left(F_{1}^{2}-\frac{q^{2}}{4M^{2}}F_{2}^{2}\right)

(A.7)

\displaystyle-[(p^{\prime}_{\mu}-p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}-p_{\nu})+2g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})](F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}

A.3
Differential Phase Space

The general expression for the differential cross section for two-body scattering is given by (see Equation
1.32
):

\displaystyle d\Phi

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(k+p-k^{\prime}-p^{\prime})\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{k^{\prime}}}{(2\pi)^{3}2E^{\prime}_{e}}\frac{d^{3}\mathbf{p^{\prime}}}{(2\pi)^{3}2E^{\prime}_{N}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{\delta^{0}(E_{e}+M-E^{\prime}_{e}-E^{\prime}_{N})}{E^{\prime}_{e}E^{\prime}_{N}}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k^{\prime}}-\mathbf{p^{\prime}})d^{3}\mathbf{p^{\prime}}d^{3}\mathbf{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{\delta^{0}\left(E_{e}+M-E^{\prime}_{e}-\sqrt{(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k^{\prime}})^{2}+M^{2}}\right)}{E^{\prime}_{e}\sqrt{(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k^{\prime}})^{2}+M^{2}}}{E^{\prime}_{e}}^{2}dE^{\prime}_{e}d\Omega

(A.8)

The last line has been obtained by integrating over the outgoing nucleon momentum (
\mathbf{p^{\prime}}
). Here,
|\mathbf{k}|=E_{e}
and
|\mathbf{k^{\prime}}|=E^{\prime}_{e}
1
1

1

Neglecting the rest mass of the electron.
, therefore,

(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k^{\prime}})^{2}=E^{2}_{e}+{E^{\prime}_{e}}^{2}-2E_{e}E_{e}^{\prime}\cos\theta_{e}

(A.9)

Now, considering

z=E^{\prime}_{e}+\sqrt{E^{2}_{e}+{E^{\prime}_{e}}^{2}-2E_{e}E_{e}^{\prime}\cos\theta_{e}+M^{2}},

(A.10)

we obtain,

\frac{dz}{dE^{\prime}_{e}}=\frac{z-E_{e}\cos\theta_{e}}{\sqrt{z-E^{\prime}_{e}}}

(A.11)

Substituting (
A.9
), (
A.10
), and (
A.11
) into (
A.8
), we obtain,

\displaystyle d\Phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{\delta^{0}(E_{e}+M-z)}{E^{\prime}_{e}(z-E_{e}\cos\theta_{e})}{E^{\prime}_{e}}^{2}dzd\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\frac{{E^{\prime}_{e}}^{2}}{E^{\prime}_{e}(E_{e}+M-E_{e}\cos\theta_{e})}d\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}M}\frac{E^{\prime}_{e}}{E_{e}}\left(\frac{E_{e}}{1+\frac{E_{e}}{M}(1-\cos\theta_{e})}\right)d\Omega

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}M}\frac{E^{\prime 2}_{e}}{E_{e}}d\Omega

(A.12)

This is the desired expression for the differential phase space in the lab frame for two-body scattering.

A.4
Spin Averaged Squared Amplitude

The expression for the spin averaged squared amplitude
\langle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\rangle
is given by Equation
1.2.4
as:

\displaystyle\langle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{4}}{q^{4}}L^{\mu\nu}_{e}W^{N}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2e^{4}}{q^{4}}(k^{\mu}k^{\prime\nu}+k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\times\bigg\{\bigg.(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})\left(F_{1}^{2}-\frac{q^{2}}{4M^{2}}F_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad-[(p^{\prime}_{\mu}-p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}-p_{\nu})+2g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})](F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\bigg.\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{2e^{4}}{q^{4}}\left[\mathcal{A}\left(F_{1}^{2}-\frac{q^{2}}{4M^{2}}F_{2}^{2}\right)-\mathcal{B}(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\right]

(A.13)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=(k^{\mu}k^{\prime\nu}+k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime})(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})

(A.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=(k^{\mu}k^{\prime\nu}+k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime})[(p^{\prime}_{\mu}-p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}-p_{\nu})+2g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})]

We will evaluate these coefficients separately for convenience.

Evaluating
\mathcal{A}

\mathcal{A}=(\underbrace{k^{\mu}k^{\prime\nu}}_{(1)}+\underbrace{k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}}_{(2)}-\underbrace{g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime}}_{(3)})\underbrace{(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})}_{(4)}

(A.15)

(1)\times(4)
:

\displaystyle k^{\mu}k^{\prime\nu}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})

\displaystyle=(k\cdot p^{\prime}+k\cdot p)(k^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}+k^{\prime}\cdot p)

(A.16)

\displaystyle=\left(2ME_{e}+\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)\left(2ME_{e}+\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:labframe}]

(2)\times(4)
:

\displaystyle k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})

\displaystyle=(k^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}+k^{\prime}\cdot p)(k\cdot p^{\prime}+k\cdot p)

(A.17)

\displaystyle=\left(2ME_{e}+\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)\left(2ME_{e}+\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:labframe}]

(3)\times(4)
:

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}+p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})

\displaystyle=k\cdot k^{\prime}(p^{\prime\nu}+p^{\nu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}+p_{\nu})

(A.18)

\displaystyle=k\cdot k^{\prime}(p^{\prime}+p)^{2}

\displaystyle=k\cdot k^{\prime}(2M^{2}+2p\cdot p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{q^{2}}{2}(4M^{2}-q^{2})\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:labframe}]

Now, substituting (
A.16
), (
A.17
), and (
A.18
) into (
A.15
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=(1)\times(4)+(2)\times(4)-(3)\times(4)

\displaystyle=2\left(2ME_{e}+\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{q^{2}}{2}(4M^{2}-q^{2})

\displaystyle=8M^{2}E_{e}^{2}+4ME_{e}(-2ME_{e}+2ME^{\prime}_{e})+2M^{2}\left(-4E_{e}E^{\prime}_{e}\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}\right)\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:labframe}]

\displaystyle=8M^{2}E_{e}E^{\prime}_{e}\cos^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}

\displaystyle=-2M^{2}\frac{\cos^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}{\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}\left(-4E_{e}E^{\prime}_{e}\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-2M^{2}q^{2}\frac{\cos^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}{\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}

(A.19)

Evaluating
\mathcal{B}

\mathcal{B}=(\underbrace{k^{\mu}k^{\prime\nu}}_{(1)}+\underbrace{k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}}_{(2)}-\underbrace{g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime}}_{(3)})[\underbrace{(p^{\prime}_{\mu}-p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}-p_{\nu})}_{(4)}+\underbrace{2g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})}_{(5)}]

(A.20)

(1)\times(4)
:

\displaystyle k^{\mu}k^{\prime\nu}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}-p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}-p_{\nu})

\displaystyle=(k\cdot p^{\prime}-k\cdot p)(k^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\cdot p)

(A.21)

(2)\times(4)
:

\displaystyle k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}-p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}-p_{\nu})

\displaystyle=(k^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\cdot p)(k\cdot p^{\prime}-k\cdot p)

(A.22)

(3)\times(4)
:

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime}(p^{\prime}_{\mu}-p_{\mu})(p^{\prime}_{\nu}-p_{\nu})

\displaystyle=k\cdot k^{\prime}(p^{\prime}-p)^{2}

(A.23)

(1)\times(5)
:

k^{\mu}k^{\prime\mu}\,2g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})=2k\cdot k^{\prime}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})

(A.24)

(2)\times(5)
:

k^{\prime\mu}k^{\nu}\,2g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})=2k\cdot k^{\prime}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})

(A.25)

(3)\times(5)
:

g^{\mu\nu}k\cdot k^{\prime}\,2g_{\mu\nu}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})=8k\cdot k^{\prime}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})

(A.26)

Now, substituting (
A.21
), (
A.22
), (
A.23
), (
A.24
), (
A.25
), and (
A.26
) into (
A.20
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle=(1)\times(4)+(2)\times(4)-(3)\times(4)+(1)\times(5)+(2)\times(5)-(3)\times(5)

\displaystyle=2(k^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\cdot p)(k\cdot p^{\prime}-k\cdot p)-k\cdot k^{\prime}(p^{\prime}-p)^{2}-4k\cdot k^{\prime}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})

\displaystyle=2k^{\prime}\cdot(p^{\prime}-p)\,k\cdot(p^{\prime}-p)-k\cdot k^{\prime}(p^{\prime}-p)^{2}-4k\cdot k^{\prime}(p\cdot p^{\prime}-M^{2})

\displaystyle=2\left(-\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)-\left(-\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)(q^{2})-4\left(-\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{q^{2}}{2}\right)\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:labframe}]

\displaystyle=-q^{4}

(A.27)

Coming Back to
\langle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\rangle

Substituting (
A.19
) and (
A.27
) into (
A.4
), we obtain,

\displaystyle\langle|\mathcal{M}|^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{4}}{q^{4}}L^{\mu\nu}_{e}W^{N}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{2e^{4}}{q^{4}}\left[-2M^{2}q^{2}\frac{\cos^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}{\sin^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}\left(F_{1}^{2}-\frac{q^{2}}{4M^{2}}F_{2}^{2}\right)+q^{4}(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{4}M^{2}\cos^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}{E_{e}E^{\prime}_{e}\sin^{4}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}}\left[\left(F_{1}^{2}-\frac{q^{2}}{4M^{2}}F_{2}^{2}\right)-\frac{q^{2}}{2M^{2}}(F_{1}+F_{2})^{2}\tan^{2}\frac{\theta_{e}}{2}\right]\qquad[\text{using}\,\,\eqref{eqn:appena:labframe}]

(A.28)

This is the desired expression for the spin averaged squared amplitude in the lab frame for the unpolarized elastic
eN
scattering in one-photon exchange (OPE) approximation.
